# Video Title: Shark Tank's All-Time Records Revealed | Shark Tank US | Shark Tank Global

[0:00] "Shark Tank" is one of the longest-running
and most-watched reality shows of all time.
[0:05] It's been a stalwart of ABC's lineup since
2009.
[0:08] The show lets viewers in on the mechanics,
struggles, and triumphs of starting and operating
[0:13] a business.
[0:13] Thousands of entrepreneurs have entered the
tank and pleaded their case to the likes of
[0:17] Mark Cuban, Barbara Corcoran, Daymond John,
Lori Grenier, Robert Herjavec, Kevin O'Leary,
[0:24] and more, hoping they'll invest in their burgeoning
companies.
[0:27] Not everyone got what they wanted, though:
[0:29] "I'm sorry.
[0:31] I'm not going to take the journey with you.
[0:33] I'm out."
[0:34] Whether they made a deal or not, some made
for some excellent television and placed themselves
[0:38] in the storied history of the business-minded
reality series.
[0:41] Here are the people who set all-time "Shark
Tank" records.
[0:45] There's no age limit on inventing cool stuff
nor starting a business to sell it.
[0:50] To honor the youthful creative spirit, "Shark
Tank" aired a special kids-only episode in
[0:54] March 2014.
[0:56] Every entrepreneur who walked on the set was
well below the legal voting age, particularly
[1:00] Kiowa Kavovit.
[1:02] She stood on a stool to be better visible
to the TV cameras and pitched Boo Boo Goo,
[1:06] a liquid adhesive bandage system.
[1:09] She came up with the concept at the age of
four, and Kavovit had a pending patent for
[1:12] her invention at the time.
[1:14] The substance forms a hard, protective surface
over skin and wounds that, unlike standard
[1:19] bandages, doesn't peel off early.
[1:21] She added,
[1:22] "I never liked Band-Aids, and I didn't want
people to see that I had something.
[1:26] 'Cause look at the board.
[1:28] There's Boo-Boo Goo, there's the other paint-on
bandage, and there's the Band-Aid."
[1:33] The young entrepreneur consulted with her
business partner, her father, and took a deal
[1:37] from Kevin Harrington for $100,000 for a 25
percent equity stake.
[1:42] When Kavovit taped her "Shark Tank" episode,
she was just six years old, making her the
[1:46] youngest businessperson to get a deal in "Shark
Tank'' history.
[1:50] In the eighth season of "Shark Tank," the
show welcomed a clever and unique product
[1:53] pitch from Don Wildman, an accomplished entrepreneur
and athlete.
[1:58] The founder of the gym chain that eventually
became Bally's Total Fitness, Wildman competed
[2:02] in triathlons into his eighties and frequently
skied.
[2:05] "I love all kinds of sports.
[2:06] I've done the Ironman nine times."
[2:08] "You've done the Ironman nine times?"
[2:10] "He won it once."
[2:11] His athletic endeavors inspired the product
he pitched on "Shark Tank" with partner Jake
[2:15] Sullivan: Hand Out Gloves.
[2:17] They're hand coverings that offer warmth and
protection from the wintry elements, while
[2:21] also allowing the wearer to quickly expose
their fingers to perform small tasks.
[2:25] Barbara Corcoran offered a $300,000 line of
credit for a 25 percent equity stake in Wildman
[2:31] and Sullivan's company.
[2:32] At the time of his appearance on "Shark Tank,"
Wildman was 83, making him the oldest person
[2:37] ever to pitch to the sharks.
[2:39] Sadly, Wildman passed away at age 85 in 2018.
[2:44] According to Shark Robert Herjavec, each roughly
40-minute episode of "Shark Tank" is compiled
[2:49] from footage obtained over 12 hours of shooting.
[2:52] During a taping day, the Sharks entertain
around eight entrepreneurs' pitches, each
[2:56] consisting of about an hour of presentation,
questioning, and negotiation.
[3:00] Each "Shark Tank" segment is edited down to
about 10 minutes, so viewers have no idea
[3:04] how long it actually takes a business owner
to attempt to sell the Sharks on their idea.
[3:08] Some take longer than others.
[3:10] In season four, Michael Tsang appeared on

[3:14] that created an air-tight vacuum seal when
placed over leftovers to keep them fresh longer.
[3:19] Mark Cuban and Lori Greiner teamed up to grab

[3:24] a $90,000 investment, but that's not the big

[3:48] Tenacity, perseverance, and picking oneself

[3:52] virtues that "Shark Tank" is all about.
[3:55] While the sharks accept at least one or two

[3:59] just as many, sending entrepreneurs in need

[4:03] scrambling for plan B. For Kim Meckwood, the

[4:08] seven trips to "Shark Tank."
[4:10] With that many attempts to make a deal, Meckwood

[4:15] Meckwood created the Click and Carry, a small,
cheap-to-manufacture gadget that helps folks
[4:20] comfortably carry multiple grocery bags all
at once.
[4:23] The inventor auditioned for "Shark Tank" seven

[4:27] — which she came up with in a very interesting

[4:41] Barbara Corcoran countered, wanting an 85

[4:51] up with the $225,000 for 40 percent of Click

[4:56] According to the trade publication The Hustle,
the sharks heard nearly 900 pitches over the
[5:00] first ten seasons of "Shark Tank," with about
500 deals reached.
[5:04] Typically, an agreement was worth about $238,000

[5:10] But a December 2014 episode featured a deal

[5:24] consumed.

[6:02] When the sharks aren't buying entrepreneur's
